    44	export function normalizeImport(fromFile, spec) {
    45	  if (!spec.startsWith(".")) return spec;
    46	  const parts = fromFile.split("/").slice(0, -1);
    47	  for (const part of spec.split("/")) {
    48	    if (!part || part === ".") continue;
    49	    if (part === "..") parts.pop();
    50	    else parts.push(part);
    51	  }
    52	  return parts.join("/");
    53	}
    54	
    55	export async function boundaryViolations(files) {
    56	  const rows = [];
    57	  const re = /\bimport(?:\s+[^"'()]+?\s+from\s+|\s*\(\s*)(["'])([^"']+)\1/g;
    58	  for (const file of files) {
    59	    const fromLayer = layerOf(file);
    60	    const lines = (await Deno.readTextFile(file).catch(() => "")).split(
    61	      /\r?\n/,
    62	    );
    63	    for (let i = 0; i < lines.length; i++) {
    64	      re.lastIndex = 0;
    65	      let match;
    66	      while ((match = re.exec(lines[i]))) {
    67	        const target = normalizeImport(file, match[2]);
    68	        const toLayer = layerOf(target);
    69	        const bad = (fromLayer === "rules" &&
    70	          (toLayer === "display" || toLayer === "bridge")) ||
    71	          (fromLayer === "display" && toLayer === "rules") ||
    72	          (fromLayer === "shared" && toLayer !== "shared" &&
    73	            toLayer !== "other");
    74	        if (bad) {
    75	          rows.push({
    76	            file,
    77	            line: i + 1,
    78	            text: `${fromLayer} -> ${toLayer}: ${match[2]}`,
    79	          });
    80	        }
    81	      }
    82	    }
    83	  }
    84	  return rows;
    85	}

   151	    kind: classifySystemCall(row),
   152	  }));
   153	  const directSystemCalls = systemCallNotes.filter((row) =>
   154	    row.kind === "possible-violation"
   155	  );
   156	  const boundaries = await boundaryViolations(src);
   157	  const systems = await systemCounts();
   158	  return {
   159	    src,
   160	    systems,
   161	    emitSafe,
   162	    rulesNondeterminism: nondeterminismHazards,
   163	    generationAsync,
   164	    directSystemCalls,
   165	    systemCallNotes,
   166	    boundaries,
   167	  };
   168	}
   169	
   170	export function formatHealthReport(report) {
   171	  const {
   172	    src,
   173	    systems,
   174	    emitSafe,
   175	    rulesNondeterminism,
   176	    generationAsync = [],
   177	    directSystemCalls,
   178	    systemCallNotes = directSystemCalls,
   179	    boundaries,
   180	  } = report;
   181	  const lines = [];
   182	  lines.push("agent health");
   183	  lines.push(`files scanned: ${src.length}`);
   184	  lines.push(
   185	    `systems: ${systems.registered} registered / ${systems.files} files`,
   186	  );
   187	  lines.push(`emitSafe refs: ${emitSafe.length}`);
   188	  lines.push(`rules nondeterminism hazards: ${rulesNondeterminism.length}`);
   189	  lines.push(`generation async allowances: ${generationAsync.length}`);
   190	  lines.push(`layer boundary violations: ${boundaries.length}`);
   191	  lines.push(`possible system-to-system calls: ${directSystemCalls.length}`);
   192	  const sections = [
   193	    ["emitSafe refs", emitSafe],
   194	    ["rules nondeterminism hazards", rulesNondeterminism],
   195	    ["generation async allowances", generationAsync],
   196	    ["layer boundary violations", boundaries],
   197	    ["system call classifications", systemCallNotes],
   198	  ];
   199	  for (const [title, rows] of sections) {
   200	    if (!rows.length) continue;
   201	    lines.push("");
   202	    lines.push(title + ":");
   203	    for (const row of rows.slice(0, 30)) {
   204	      const prefix = row.kind ? `[${row.kind}] ` : "";
   205	      lines.push(`  ${row.file}:${row.line} ${prefix}${row.text}`);
   206	    }
   207	    if (rows.length > 30) lines.push(`  ... ${rows.length - 30} more`);
   208	  }
   209	  return lines.join("\n") + "\n";
   210	}
   211	
   212	export async function main() {
   213	  const report = await buildHealthReport();
   214	  console.log(formatHealthReport(report).trimEnd());
   215	}
   216	
   217	if (import.meta.main) await main();

     1	# The Ten Commandments of JSHack
     2	
     3	These exist because the same mistake was made **twice** (Oct 23 and Nov 6, 2025).
     4	Both times the project was derailed by the same pattern: chasing elegant engine
     5	work instead of building the game.
     6	
     7	---
     8	
     9	## I. Thou shalt not build a rendering engine.
    10	
    11	JSHack is a **roguelike**. It uses a tile grid. Tiles are integers. Movement is
    12	cardinal/diagonal on integer coordinates. There is no continuous space, no SDF
    13	kernels, no analytic geometry. If you are writing `Math.hypot` for collision
    14	detection, stop.
    15	
    16	## II. Thou shalt not build a lighting engine.
    17	
    18	No raycasting. No shadow volumes. No specular fields. No emissive light polygons.
    19	No smooth glow renderers. FOV is a **boolean visibility mask** — each tile is
    20	either visible, remembered, or unknown. Use simple shadowcasting (roguelike
    21	standard) when the time comes. If a file has "kernel" in its name, delete it.
    22	
    23	## III. Thou shalt keep positions as integers.
    24	
    25	`Position.x` and `Position.y` are integers. `MoveIntent.dx` and `MoveIntent.dy`
    26	are -1, 0, or 1. There is no `BoundingCircle`. There is no `strideDistance`.
    27	There is no `distanceMove()`. Adjacency is Manhattan or Chebyshev distance === 1.
    28	
    29	## IV. Thou shalt ship gameplay before graphics.
    30	
    31	Every feature must answer: "what does the player **do** differently?" If the
    32	answer is "nothing, but it looks better," it waits. Combat, items, spells,
    33	monsters, dungeon layouts, progression — these come first. Visual polish is
    34	a reward for finishing systems, not a substitute for them.
    35	
    36	## V. Thou shalt write tests before systems.
    37	
    38	If a system has no test, it doesn't exist. The Nov 1-4 rebuild succeeded because
    39	it was test-driven. The Oct 23 and Nov 6 derailments had zero tests. Tests are
    40	the guardrail that keeps you honest about what actually works.
    41	
    42	## VI. Thou shalt recognize the commit message canary.
    43	
    44	When commit messages degrade to single letters ("a", "a", "a"), you are in a
    45	flow state solving the wrong problem. Stop. `git stash`. Walk away. Come back
    46	and ask: "is this a game feature or an engine feature?" If it's engine, kill it.
    47	
    48	## VII. Thou shalt not exceed one new system per session.
    49	
    50	One ECS system per working session. Wire it, test it, commit it with a real
    51	message. If you're adding 500+ lines across 25 files in one commit, you've
    52	lost the plot. Small, tested, incremental.
    53	
    54	## VIII. Thou shalt keep the rules layer pure.
    55	
    56	`src/rules/` must never import from `src/display/` or reference rendering
    57	concepts. Rules operate on integer grids, entity queries, and intent resolution.
    58	If a rules-layer file needs a "geometry kernel," the architecture is wrong.
    59	
    60	## IX. Thou shalt not solve problems the player cannot see.
    61	
    62	Sub-tile collision resolution, capsule sweep tests, continuous-space pathfinding —
    63	the player sees a grid. Solve grid problems. The elegance of the solution is
    64	irrelevant if it solves a problem that doesn't exist in the game the player plays.
    65	
    66	## X. Thou shalt drive systems with data, not code.
    67	
    68	Monster behaviors, item effects, material reactions, status procs — these belong
    69	in declarative definition files under `src/rules/data/`, not in `if/else` chains
    70	inside systems. Systems read data and execute it; they do not *know* what "poison"
    71	does. When adding a new effect or behavior, the first file you touch should be a
    72	data definition, and it should have a schema validated at boot. If you're adding a
    73	case to a switch statement, you're hard-coding.
    74	
    75	## XI. Thou shalt remember: you did this twice.
    76	
    77	Oct 23, 2025: 564 lines of lighting infrastructure in one evening. Deleted
    78	12,924 lines six days later. Nov 6, 2025: 921-line GeometryKernel reintroduced
    79	two days after the clean rebuild. Same mistake. Same rabbit hole. Different hat.
    80	
    81	If you are excited about the math, **that is the warning sign.**
    82	
    83	## XII. Thou shalt not build a second scheduler.
    84	
    85	ECS-js already owns deferred structural mutation (`world.command`) and tick order.
    86	Do not recreate that engine layer inside `src/rules/`.
    87	
    88	If you need turn flow or cross-system ordering:
    89	- use intent components
    90	- use scheduler phases
    91	- use events
    92	
    93	If you need all-or-nothing resolution for one action (e.g., cancel item use):
    94	- use action-local transaction helpers only
    95	- keep them local to action contexts
    96	- never let them become a global queue

```

> AGENT

The repo also makes its anti-pattern history explicit, which matters for agentic development: it gives future agents negative examples and stop conditions, not just positive architecture docs. That reduces the chance that an agent “improves” the wrong thing.

     1	# Separation Manifest
     2	
     3	Authoritative guide for keeping the deterministic simulation clean and all presentation flexible. Thinking is cheap; code is expensive.
     4	
     5	## Top-level layout
     6	
     7	/src/
     8	  rules/      — pure deterministic simulation
     9	  bridge/     — neutral contract (snapshots, events, readers)
    10	  display/    — rendering, VFX, lighting, camera, input
    11	  scenes/     — optional orchestration (camera cues, flow)
    12	  shared/     — pure utilities (math, types)
    13	  app/        — composition roots (playable, headless)
    14	reference/
    15	tests/
    16	
    17	## Hard boundaries (who can import whom)
    18	
    19	- rules/ may import: shared/
    20	  - MUST NOT import: bridge/, display/, scenes/, DOM, timers, rAF
    21	- bridge/ may import: rules/ (read-only selectors), shared/
    22	  - Exposes: schema/ (DTOs), readers/ (snapshot builders), codecs/
    23	  - MUST NOT import: display/, scenes/
    24	- display/ may import: bridge/ (schema + readers), shared/
    25	  - MUST NOT import: rules/
    26	- scenes/ may import: bridge/, display/ (signals/director APIs), shared/, a tiny rulesApi façade provided by app/
    27	  - MUST NOT import: rules/ internals directly
    28	- app/ wires everything; owns rulesApi and lifecycle; no game logic
    29	
    30	If in doubt: rules → bridge → display. Never the reverse.
    31	
    32	## Determinism policy
    33	
    34	- Only app/scenes call SimClock.step(dt). No rAF or timers exist in rules/.
    35	- Given the same seed and intent sequence, the sim produces identical state/events.
    36	- Visual randomness is allowed in display/ but never feeds back into rules/.
    37	
    38	## Camera policy
    39	
    40	- Camera is a display resource (not an ECS component): display/camera/*
    41	- One primary camera controlling world→screen transform (x, y, scale).
    42	- Cinematics (jump, ease, follow, shake, zoom) are driven by display-side director.
    43	
    44	## Lighting policy
    45	
    46	- All lighting math lives under display/lighting/.
    47	- rules/ only exposes semantic tags (e.g., Tag_Emissive); no radii, colors, or falloff.
    48	- display derives light sources from snapshot emissives and computes light fields.
    49	
    50	## Bridge: data contract
    51	
    52	Schema shapes (stable, serializable, read-only):
    53	
    54	WorldView
    55	{
    56	  turn: number,
    57	  seed: number,
    58	  player: { id: number, pos: { x: number, y: number } } | null,
    59	  invulnTurns: number,
    60	  entities: Array<{
    61	    id: number,
    62	    kind: string,          // archetype or semantic kind
    63	    pos: { x: number, y: number },
    64	    tags: string[]         // semantic tags only
    65	  }>,
    66	  solids: Array<{ id: number, x: number, y: number }>,
    67	  emissives: Array<{ id: number, x: number, y: number, kind: string }>
    68	}
    69	
    70	Events (semantic only; no pixels/brightness):
    71	- moved { id, from:{x,y}, to:{x,y} }
    72	- damage { id, amount, at:{x,y} }
    73	- died { id, at:{x,y} }
    74	- spawned { id, at:{x,y}, kind }
    75	
    76	No light.* events. Lighting is visual-side logic.
    77	
    78	Readers (examples):
    79	- readers/world_view.build(world): WorldView
    80	- readers/spotlight.resolve(world, { id }): { x, y } | null
    81	
    82	## Display: presentation contract
    83	
    84	- Renders using only WorldView data; never mutates sim state.
    85	- Maps entity kind/tags → visuals via palette tables (glyphs, colors, styles).
    86	- Pass pipeline: tiles/glyphs → lightmask → vfx → hud.
    87	- VFX own their lifetimes in visual time; may consume bridge events.
    88	
    89	## Scenes: orchestration
    90	
    91	- Optional director that sequences camera + display cues and decides when to step the sim.
    92	- Scenes never touch ECS directly; they:
    93	  - call rulesApi (façade in app/) for spawn/name/invuln/step
    94	  - emit display signals for camera/VFX/UI
    95	
    96	Example minimal rulesApi (owned by app/):
    97	- newWorld(seed)
    98	- generateDungeon(options?)
    99	- spawnPlayer({ name }) → id
   100	- applyInvulnerable(entityId, turns)
   101	- step(dt=1)
   102	
   103	## Input flow
   104	
   105	DOM → display/input → rules/io intents → app.rulesApi.step() →
   106	bridge readers build WorldView → display renders passes.
   107	
   108	## “Blue o” rule
   109	
   110	- Glyphs/colors/animations are presentation concerns only.
   111	- Rules expose kind/tags/positions; display decides visuals.
   112	- If symbols have gameplay meaning, add a semantic component in rules/ (e.g., Symbol { code }) — still no visuals in rules/.
   113	
   114	## Quick checklist before merging
   115	
   116	- [ ] No references to DOM, window, or rAF in rules/
   117	- [ ] No imports from rules/ inside display/
   118	- [ ] Bridge exports only plain data; no live component references
   119	- [ ] Camera used only as a display resource
   120	- [ ] Lighting exists only under display/lighting/
   121	- [ ] Visual mappings (glyphs/palette) live in display/, not rules/
   122	- [ ] WorldView stable; additions happen via bridge/schema first
   123	
   124	## Notes
   125	
   126	- The “giant @” intro is camera.scale >> 1, not a different entity.
   127	- Invulnerability pulse for first N turns is a display VFX keyed off WorldView.invulnTurns.
   128	- Headless tests run purely against rules/ with deterministic seeds and intent sequences.

     1	# Runtime Topology Doctrine
     2	
     3	Runtime multiplicity is modeled as attached child entities.
     4	
     5	Components describe facts. Hierarchy describes topology. Resolvers derive views.
     6	Systems execute behavior through events.
     7	
     8	This doctrine makes explicit a pattern JSHack already uses in inventory and proc
     9	topology: when runtime things multiply, attach entities instead of hiding active
    10	objects inside arrays on one component.
    11	
    12	For the incremental migration backlog, see
    13	[RUNTIME_TOPOLOGY_WORK_ITEMS.md](RUNTIME_TOPOLOGY_WORK_ITEMS.md).
    14	
    15	## Canonical Rule
    16	
    17	If a runtime thing has identity, lifecycle, ownership, source, duration, charges,
    18	removability, targeting, behavior hooks, query relevance, debug relevance, or
    19	save/load significance, it should usually be represented as its own entity.
    20	
    21	The preferred shape is:
    22	
    23	```txt
    24	parent entity
    25	  attached runtime node
    26	    behavior node
    27	    state component
    28	    source component
    29	```
    30	
    31	The discouraged runtime shape is:
    32	
    33	```js
    34	ActiveEffects: {
    35	  effects: []
    36	}
    37	```
    38	
    39	or:
    40	
    41	```js
    42	Sockets: {
    43	  slots: []
    44	}
    45	```
    46	
    47	or:
    48	
    49	```js
    50	Enchantments: {
    51	  active: []
    52	}
    53	```
    54	
    55	Those structures hide active runtime objects inside private containers. They make
    56	the objects harder to query, inspect, remove, serialize, target, and extend.
    57	
    58	## Invariant
    59	
    60	One entity may have zero or one component of a given type.
    61	
    62	Multiplicity is represented by many entities, not many same-type components on
    63	one entity. Components remain flat state records. Definitions may be nested.
    64	Runtime state should be ECS-visible.
    65	
    66	## Rule of Thumb
    67	
    68	Arrays are acceptable for static authoring data and small private value lists.
    69	
    70	Arrays are suspicious when they contain active runtime things.
    71	
    72	If another system needs to find it, remove it, target it, tick it, save it,
    73	inspect it, or react to it, it probably deserves to be an entity.
    74	
    75	## Definitions vs Runtime
    76	
    77	Authoring data may stay nested when it describes static defaults, catalogs,
    78	weights, proc packages, or imported definitions.
    79	
    80	Runtime identity belongs in entities.
    81	
    82	Use this split:
    83	
    84	```txt
    85	Definitions
    86	  static authoring data
    87	
    88	Entities
    89	  runtime identity
    90	
    91	Components
    92	  flat facts
    93	
    94	Hierarchy
    95	  runtime topology
    96	
    97	Resolvers
    98	  derived views
    99	
   100	Systems
   101	  behavior
   102	
   103	Events
   104	  decoupling
   105	```
   106	
   107	## Reference Shape: Inventory
   108	
   109	Inventory is the reference implementation for this doctrine.
   110	
   111	```txt
   112	owner entity
   113	  InventoryRoot entity
   114	    item entity
   115	    item entity
   116	    item entity
   117	```
   118	
   119	Inventory items are real entities. Containment is expressed through hierarchy.
   120	Read/write access is mediated through the inventory facade instead of every
   121	system inventing its own traversal.
   122	
   123	New topology-backed systems should follow that pattern:
   124	
   125	- Create explicit child entities for runtime objects.
   126	- Put flat facts on those entities as components.
   127	- Expose canonical helpers or facades for common operations.
   128	- Keep compatibility views behind resolvers while migration is underway.
   129	
   130	## Reference Shape: Proc Topology
   131	
   132	Proc behavior should remain visible to ECS traversal:
   133	
   134	```txt
   135	item or status node
   136	  AffixTopologyNode / EnchantmentNode / SocketNode / StatusEffectNode
   137	    ProcNode
   138	      ActivationGate
   139	      ProcEffect
   140	      DerivedExpression
   141	```
   142	
   143	Attached behavior nodes make procs inspectable and composable. Avoid rebuilding
   144	runtime behavior from opaque arrays once a runtime node can own it.
   145	
   146	## Legacy Array Ratchet
   147	
   148	Some systems still use array-backed runtime state, especially status effects,
   149	equipment slots, affixes, enchantments, and socket state. Do not rewrite all of
   150	them at once.
   151	
   152	Use this ratchet:
   153	
   154	- New systems must follow runtime topology.
   155	- Touched systems should migrate toward runtime topology.
   156	- Legacy array-backed systems are allowed temporarily.
   157	- Legacy arrays must be treated as compatibility state, not precedent.
   158	- Resolvers and facades should isolate legacy structures.
   159	- When a runtime node exists, make it the source of truth and derive old views
   160	  from it only where compatibility requires that.
   161	
   162	## Migration Targets
   163	
   164	Best candidates for incremental migration:
   165	
   166	1. Enchantments
   167	2. Gem sockets and socketed state
   168	3. Active effects
   169	4. Equipment slots
   170	5. ItemInfo affixes
   171	
   172	The preferred status shape is:
   173	
   174	```txt
   175	actor
   176	  StatusEffectNode
   177	    Duration
   178	    Source
   179	    StatModifier
   180	    DerivedExpression

```

     1	# Agents & ecs-js
     2	
     3	`ecs-js` is written so autonomous agents, copilots, and other automated operators can understand and control simulations without fighting a game-engine stack. This document gives agents (and the humans piloting them) the context needed to reason about the runtime, stay deterministic, and iterate safely inside code-canvas or other tool-augmented environments. Think of it as the field manual that complements the API/source-of-truth in `README.md`—when you need signatures or conceptual deep dives, go there; when you need to keep the project humming, stay here.
     4	
     5	## Principles for agents
     6	
     7	### Why ecs-js works for agents
     8	- **Deterministic core**: seeded RNG (`mulberry32`) plus immediate intratick adds and deferred destructive mutations mean you can replay every tick exactly. Always pass a seed into `new World({ seed })` so your reasoning remains reproducible.
     9	- **Sharp escape hatches exist**: `removeImmediate()` / `destroyImmediate()` bypass intratick deferral for helper-local invariants. Use them rarely and deliberately; they can invalidate active iterators.
    10	- **Caller-driven ticks**: you own the loop. Drive discrete steps (`world.tick(1)`) while inspecting state, or stream `dt` from real sensors for real-time sims. No hidden scheduler.
    11	- **Phase-agnostic scripts**: declare whatever phases your workflow needs (`intent → resolve → effects`, `sense → plan → act`, etc.) and pin systems explicitly so an agent can narrate the order of operations.
    12	- **Pure logic, zero IO**: there is no rendering, DOM, or async timing, so agents can manipulate worlds from CLIs, sandboxes, notebooks, or IDE workspaces without side effects.
    13	- **Composable helpers**: builders like `World.create()` or `Systems.phase()` keep configuration declarative, which is easier for LLM-based editors to diff, patch, and explain.
    14	
    15	### Step-and-explain workflow
    16	1. `World.create()` or `new World()` with a fixed seed and store (`map` for clarity, `soa` for perf).
    17	2. Install systems/queries with explicit names so you can reference them in explanations.
    18	3. Tick once, inspect (`world.debug.inspect(id)`), log or assert what changed.
    19	4. Repeat with updated inputs or events. Because ticks are synchronous, agents can pause between steps, regenerate code, and resume without drifting state.
    20	
    21	```js
    22	import { World, PHASE_SCRIPTS } from 'ecs-js/index.js'
    23	
    24	const world = World.create({ seed: 42 })
    25	  .withPhases('sense', 'plan', 'act', PHASE_SCRIPTS)
    26	  .useScripts({ phase: PHASE_SCRIPTS })
    27	  .system(senseSystem, 'sense')
    28	  .system(planSystem, 'plan').after(senseSystem)
    29	  .system(actSystem, 'act').after(planSystem)
    30	  .build()
    31	
    32	world.tick(1) // run a single, reviewable step
    33	```
    34	
    35	### Simulation modes & loops
    36	- **Discrete / turn-based**: call `world.tick(1)` (or any scalar) manually after each planning phase. Ideal for reasoning sandboxes and roguelikes.
    37	- **Realtime**: let the host compute `dt` (e.g., RAF delta, physics step) and pass it through `world.tick(dt)`. Systems stay pure; only the caller cares about wall time.
    38	- **Scripted phases**: mix declarative schedulers with script adapters (`PHASE_SCRIPTS`, `world.script`) so an agent can inject behavior without editing core systems.
    39	- **Hybrid**: run deterministic bursts (multiple ticks in a loop) between observable checkpoints to keep logs compact while still enabling rewind-friendly seeds.
    40	
    41	### Code-canvas / realtime considerations
    42	- Work in small, reviewable diffs so a supervising human can approve each change.
    43	- Prefer scripted scenarios (tests under `tests/`) for regression detection; they double as narrated transcripts for other agents.
    44	- For realtime loops, keep an outer host (browser, node, worker, etc.) responsible for timing and let the agent only manipulate systems/data. This separation keeps `ecs-js` deterministic even when the host is not.
    45	- Use `world.emit` events for asynchronous inputs so agents can enqueue reactions without patching every system.
    46	
    47	---
    48	
    49	## Maintenance & usage playbook
    50	
    51	The rest of this file is the dual-purpose cookbook: follow it to maintain the repository, and you will automatically be using the engine the way it was designed. Whenever you need API minutiae, pull them from `README.md` or the module in question; this playbook keeps things DRY by pointing at those sources rather than repeating them.
    52	
    53	### Repository map (know where to edit)
    54	- `README.md` — design principles, terminology, and usage narrative. Update this first when behavior shifts so humans and agents stay aligned.
    55	- `core.js` — `World`, components, entity helpers, RNG glue. Primary import surface for agents wiring bespoke worlds.
    56	- `systems.js` — declarative system registry + scheduler helpers (`Systems`, `registerSystem`, `composeScheduler`).
    57	- `scripts.js` / `scriptsPhasesExtra.js` — built-in script adapters, refs, and phase presets (used by `World.create().useScripts()`).
    58	- `adapters/` — optional integration helpers (script router, timeline utilities). Scan when bridging to external tools.
    59	- `hierarchy.js` / `crossWorld.js` — parenting helpers and cross-world references for multi-scene orchestration.
    60	- `archetype.js` — prefab builder APIs (`defineArchetype`, `Archetype()` fluent). Useful when agents need to batch-spawn entities.
    61	- `tests/` — deterministic transcripts. Add scenario files here so future agents can replay and diff. Run with `deno test --allow-read tests/`.
    62	- `demo/` — runnable sandboxes that double as reality checks for API additions.
    63	
    64	### Daily maintenance loop
    65	1. **Boot a deterministic playground**
    66	   - Pick a store (`map` for clarity, `soa` for perf) and seed.
    67	   - Declare phases you can cite in PR/commit summaries (e.g., `"sense"`, `"think"`, `"act"`).
    68	   - Register systems explicitly (`world.system(fn, 'phase')` or `Systems.phase('phase').add(fn)`), mirroring the patterns documented in `README.md`.
    69	2. **Instrument a scenario before editing**
    70	   - Snapshot entity state via `world.debug.inspect(id)` before/after ticks.
    71	   - Emit events (`world.emit('damage', payload)`) to simulate inputs without editing every system.
    72	   - Route events to entities with `makeScriptRouter` when a script-per-entity pattern fits; document the route map near the system registration.
    73	3. **Make surgical changes**
    74	   - Define components/tags in isolated modules so diffs remain scoped.
    75	   - Use archetypes for repeatable spawn logic; commit both definition and usage for end-to-end traceability.
    76	   - When touching schedulers, show the intended order in prose (or include `Systems.visualizeGraph()` output) so the next agent can reason about causality quickly.
    77	4. **Regressions & transcripts**
    78	   - Drop focused repros inside `tests/` (plain JS modules are fine; `node tests/<file>.js` keeps them executable without build tooling).
    79	   - Capture expected diffs (logs/asserts) so other agents can verify behavior without re-deriving context.
    80	   - Mention test entry points in commit messages or PR notes (e.g., “repro: `node tests/turn-order.js`”).
    81	
    82	### Runtime instrumentation & safety
    83	- Enable `debug` mode to unlock `.inspect`, `.forget`, and per-entity change logs. Leave hooks in place—when disabled they are zero-cost.
    84	- Use `world.on` / `world.emit` for synchronous event routing; pair with script routers for entity-targeted signals.
    85	- Serialize or clone worlds with `serialization.js` helpers when an agent needs to branch scenarios in parallel sandboxes.
    86	- Leverage `hierarchy.js` (`attach`, `children`, `destroySubtree`) to reason about parent/child lifecycles when modeling GUIs, squads, or nested objects.
    87	- Never mutate component schemas at runtime; define them once per module so references stay stable.
    88	- Remember to `destroySubtree` or `world.destroy(id)` when cleaning up spawned entities to avoid phantom references.
    89	- When collaborating with other agents, agree on phase names and seeds to avoid divergent worlds.
    90	- Document any non-default scheduler orderings or script routers in comments/commits so the next agent can reconstruct the control flow.
    91	- Prefer additive diffs over rewrites; when removal is required, mention why in the commit/DX log to preserve shared mental models.
    92	
    93	### Documentation & release hygiene
    94	- Update `README.md` whenever new capabilities or terminology are introduced—that is the public contract.
    95	- Mirror significant changes here in `AGENTS.md` only when the maintenance workflow needs to adjust; otherwise link to the README section to avoid DRY breaks.
    96	- Keep `AGENTS.md` close to reality by noting new maintenance rituals (e.g., “run `node tests/foo.js` before touching `scripts.js`”).
    97	- Tag alpha/beta releases only after replaying key demos under identical seeds. Document the seeds in `CHANGELOG` or release notes so agents can cross-check quickly.
    98	
    99	Happy sim-building!

    99	import { defineInventoryVirtuals, installVirtuals } from "../rules/utils/inventoryVirtuals.js";
   100	import { defineDerivedStatVirtuals } from "../rules/utils/derivedStats.js";
   101	import { definePassiveBonusVirtuals } from "../rules/utils/passiveBonuses.js";
   102	import { defineTownInterpretationVirtuals } from "../rules/utils/townInterpretationVirtuals.js";
   103	import { defineShopDebtVirtuals } from "../rules/utils/shopDebt.js";
   104	import { installDispositionOffenseListeners } from "../rules/utils/disposition.js";
   105	import { installReputationOffenseListeners } from "../rules/utils/reputation.js";
   106	import { installShopLawListeners } from "../rules/utils/shopLaw.js";
   107	import { installDialogRuntime } from "../rules/dialogues/runtime.js";
   108	import { installQuestRuntime } from "../rules/quests/runtime.js";
   109	import { installStarterFetchQuestHooks } from "../rules/quests/definitions/graveyardWatch.js";
   110	import { installRatQuestHooks, ratInfestationDeathSystem } from "../rules/quests/definitions/ratInfestation.js";
   111	import { installRunContractHooks, runContractDeathSystem } from "../rules/quests/definitions/runContract.js";
   112	// Side-effect: registers script handlers at import time
   113	import "../rules/scripts/traps.js";
   114	import "../rules/scripts/monsters.js";
   115	import "../rules/data/procPackages.js";
   116	import "../rules/dialogues/townfolkDialogs.js";
   117	import { installGemSocketListener } from "../rules/data/gemSocketAffixes.js";
   118	import { installCentipedeBodyCascade } from "../rules/utils/centipedeMovement.js";
   119	import { perceptionMemorySystem } from "../rules/systems/perceptionMemorySystem.js";
   120	import { installEnchantingOpenRequestListener } from "../rules/content/enchanting/benchGame.js";
   121	import { tombstoneSystem } from "../rules/systems/tombstoneSystem.js";
   122	
   123	/**
   124	 * @param {World} world
   125	 */
   126	export function configureWorld(world) {
   127	  clearSystems();
   128	  installScriptsAPI(world);
   129	  installVirtuals(world);
   130	  defineInventoryVirtuals(world);
   131	  defineDerivedStatVirtuals(world);
   132	  definePassiveBonusVirtuals(world);
   133	  defineTownInterpretationVirtuals(world);
   134	  defineShopDebtVirtuals(world);
   135	  installDialogRuntime(world);
   136	  installQuestRuntime(world);
   137	  installStarterFetchQuestHooks(world);
   138	  installRatQuestHooks(world);
   139	  installRunContractHooks(world);
   140	
   141	  installTownfolkDoorListener(world);
   142	  installBellListener(world);
   143	  installGemSocketListener(world);
   144	  // Install engraving scramble-on-step listener once per world
   145	  installEngraveListeners(world);
   146	  // Install bump-interact listener for immediate interactions (doors, chests, NPCs)
   147	  installBumpInteractListener(world);
   148	  // Install bump-attack listener for immediate melee-on-bump resolution
   149	  installBumpAttackListener(world);
   150	  // Install data-driven combat interaction rules (blessed vs undead, frozen shatter, etc.)
   151	  installCombatInteractions(world);
   152	  // Install taunt listeners once per world
   153	  installTauntListener(world);
   154	  // Install threat listeners once per world
   155	  installThreatListeners(world);
   156	  // Auto-pickup currency etc. when any actor moves onto a tile (reacts to "moved" event)
   157	  installMoveAutoPickupListener(world);
   158	  // Shop-law ledger catches value extraction that bypasses ordinary doorway blocking.
   159	  installShopLawListeners(world);
   160	  // Social offense memory feeds disposition first; aggro is a tactical output.
   161	  installDispositionOffenseListeners(world);
   162	  // Public reputation is downstream of witnessed or ledgered disposition changes.
   163	  installReputationOffenseListeners(world);
   164	  // Tile step effects: ice slides, lava scorch, water extinguish (reacts to "moved" event)
   165	  installTileStepEffectListener(world);
   166	  // Trap plates trigger from arrival movement events, then resolve in trapSystem.
   167	  world.install(trapStepListenerExtension);
   168	  // Material reactions consume semantic reaction events (water splash/dip, etc.).
   169	  world.install(materialReactionListenersExtension);
   170	  // Polymorph requests (e.g. mimic reveal on touch).
   171	  installPolymorphListener(world);
   172	  installCurseHooks(world);
   173	  installGenocideListener(world);
   174	  installTamingListener(world);
   175	  // Witnesses react to stealth offense and enter hunting with attacker last-known position.
   176	  installAggroFromStealthOffenseListener(world);
   177	  installFishingAction(world);
   178	  // Centipede body segments cascade position when the head moves.
   179	  installCentipedeBodyCascade(world);
   180	  installEnchantingOpenRequestListener(world);
   181	
   182	  // Phase: ai (intent producers — added intents are visible to later phases
   183	  // in the same tick because ecs-js add() is intratick-immediate)
   184	
   185	  // Jump scare triggers on first proximity to dangerous creatures (dragons, lich, etc.)
   186	  registerSystem(jumpScareSystem, 'ai');
   187	  // Flying AI claims the action with FlyIntent before scurry/chase.
   188	  registerSystem(aiFlyingSystem, 'ai');
   189	  // Scurry before chase: dumb idle creatures set a random MoveIntent which
   190	  // aiChaseSystem's existing intent-skip guard then honours.
   191	  registerSystem(aiScurrySystem, 'ai');
   192	  registerSystem(aiFarmAnimalSystem, 'ai');
   193	  registerSystem(aiTownfolkSystem, 'ai');
   194	  registerSystem(socialAggroSystem, 'ai');
   195	  registerSystem(aiChaseSystem, 'ai');
   196	  // Policy override: neural net replaces/improves intents for intel ≥ 7 monsters.
   197	  // Runs after chase so alert state and whileLOS hooks are already resolved.
   198	  registerSystem(aiPolicySystem, 'ai');
   199	  // Weapon pickup after chase so the monster's hunt state is settled first.
   200	  registerSystem(aiWeaponPickupSystem, 'ai');
   201	  registerSystem(aiScrollPickupSystem, 'ai');
   202	  registerSystem(aiScrollUseSystem, 'ai');
   203	  // Corpse eating after weapon pickup — idle scavengers and tactical devourers.
   204	  registerSystem(aiCorpseEatSystem, 'ai');
   205	  registerSystem(summonedBehaviorSystem, 'ai');
   206	  registerSystem(petCommandSystem, 'ai');
   207	  registerSystem(petBehaviorSystem, 'ai');
   208	
   209	  // Phase: intents (intent consumers + steering)
   210	  registerSystem(intentValidationSystem, 'intents', {
   211	    before: [knockbackSystem, flyIntentSystem, waitSystem, movementSystem, combatSystem],
   212	  });
   213	  // Knockback resolves before standard movement so positions are committed first.
   214	  registerSystem(knockbackSystem, 'intents', { before: [movementSystem] });
   215	  registerSystem(flyIntentSystem, 'intents');
   216	  registerSystem(waitSystem, 'intents');
   217	  registerSystem(searchSystem, 'intents');
   218	  registerSystem(postureIntentSystem, 'intents');
   219	  registerSystem(praySystem, 'intents');
   220	  registerSystem(drinkSystem, 'intents');
   221	  registerSystem(useItemSystem, 'intents');
   222	  registerSystem(applySystem, 'intents');
   223	  registerSystem(equipItemSystem, 'intents');
   224	  registerSystem(itemDropSystem, 'intents');
   225	  registerSystem(engraveSystem, 'intents');
   226	  // Shopkeeper system must run BEFORE movementSystem to block exits
   227	  registerSystem(shopkeeperSystem, 'intents');
   228	  // Taunt steering can override enemy movement intents before movement resolves.
   229	  registerSystem(tauntSteeringSystem, 'intents');
   230	  // Refill dry fountains at cooldown before interaction tries to drink.
   231	  registerSystem(fountainRegrowthSystem, 'intents');
   232	  // Resolve movement before targeted ranged actions so same-tick attacks use
   233	  // the destination a target actually reaches this turn.
   234	  registerSystem(movementSystem, 'intents', {
   235	    after: [knockbackSystem, shopkeeperSystem, tauntSteeringSystem, fountainRegrowthSystem],
   236	    before: [throwSystem, rangedAttackSystem, channelingSystem, castSpellSystem, interactionSystem, combatSystem],
   237	  });
   238	  registerSystem(throwSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem] });
   239	  registerSystem(rangedAttackSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem] });
   240	  registerSystem(attackDirectionSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem], before: [combatSystem] });
   241	  registerSystem(channelingSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem] });   // countdown before castSpellSystem fires
   242	  registerSystem(castSpellSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem, channelingSystem] });
   243	  // interactionSystem must run AFTER movementSystem: bump-to-interact adds
   244	  // InteractIntent during movement; processing it in the same tick prevents
   245	  // the shop overlay from re-firing on every subsequent action.
   246	  registerSystem(interactionSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem] });
   247	  registerSystem(combatSystem, 'intents', { after: [movementSystem] });
   248	  // Run pickup after movement so stepping onto items can pick them up immediately
   249	  registerSystem(itemPickupSystem, 'intents');
   250	  // Disarm attempts resolve before traps trigger (so disarming prevents stepping-trigger)
   251	  registerSystem(disarmTrapSystem, 'intents');
   252	  // Traps trigger after movement (player steps onto trap tile)
   253	  registerSystem(trapSystem, 'intents');
   254	
   255	  // Phase: effects (derived first, then per-turn effects)
   256	  // Weight derivation: bottom-up recomputation of Weight.total for bags/actors.
   257	  registerSystem(weightDerivationSystem, 'effects');
   258	  // Encumbrance recomputed after equipment + weight are settled; movement reads it next tick.
   259	  registerSystem(encumbranceSystem, 'effects');
   260	  // Sound propagation checks SoundEmitter vs Anatomy.hearing; updates AggroState.

     1	# Content Authoring Guide
     2	
     3	Items and monsters live in `src/content/`. One call registers everything: catalog
     4	entry, glyph/palette, hooks. No secondary registration step, no parallel data
     5	structure to keep in sync.
     6	
     7	---
     8	
     9	## Why this matters
    10	
    11	Before Phase A, items existed in two places simultaneously:
    12	
    13	- `src/rules/data/itemCatalogEquipment.js` — equipment, raw object arrays
    14	- `src/rules/data/itemCatalogMagic.js` — potions, scrolls, rings, etc.
    15	
    16	Both files were ~3500+ lines of object literals with no shared structure, no
    17	validation, and hooks scattered through inline closures. Adding a new item meant
    18	picking the right file, matching the implicit schema by hand, and hoping nothing
    19	was silently missing.
    20	
    21	The content DSL (`src/content/define.js`) collapses that into a single function
    22	call that validates inputs, infers catalog kind from type, compiles hooks, and
    23	registers palette — atomically. The old files are now empty stubs.
    24	
    25	**Benefits in practice:**
    26	
    27	- **One place.** `defineItem('my_ring', {...})` is the entire item. No second file,
    28	  no merge step, no registration call.
    29	- **Caught early.** Missing `name` or `type` throws at import time, not at the
    30	  moment the item tries to spawn.
    31	- **Hooks are first-class.** `onUse`, `onDrink`, `onThrow`, `onHit`, etc. are
    32	  top-level fields — not nested inside `hooks: { on_use: fn }` buried in a 3000-
    33	  line file.
    34	- **Glyph and catalog travel together.** No drift between palette and catalog IDs.
    35	- **Tree-shakeable by file.** Each category (`rings.js`, `weapons.js`, `potions.js`)
    36	  is its own module. Barrel import via `index.js`. Tests can import only what they need.
    37	
    38	---
    39	
    40	## File layout
    41	
    42	```
    43	src/content/items/
    44	  index.js              ← barrel import (import this, not individuals)
    45	  weapons.js
    46	  rangedWeapons.js
    47	  armors.js   helms.js  boots.js  legs.js  gloves.js  belts.js
    48	  rings.js    necks.js  offhands.js
    49	  potions.js  scrolls.js  wands.js  spellbooks.js
    50	  economy.js            ← ingredients, food, materials, fuel, junk
    51	  dawnbreaker.js        ← unique/scripted items get their own file
    52	  fishingRod.js
    53	  ...
    54	
    55	src/content/monsters/
    56	  barrowWight.js        ← scripted monsters get their own file
    57	```
    58	
    59	**Rule:** pure-data items go in the category file. Items with significant scripted
    60	behavior (unique weapons, artifacts, multi-hook consumables) get their own file.
    61	
    62	---
    63	
    64	## defineItem()
    65	
    66	```js
    67	import { defineItem } from '../content/define.js';
    68	
    69	defineItem('ring_endurance', {
    70	  name:        'Ring of Endurance',
    71	  type:        'ring',                // drives catalogKind + slot inference
    72	  glyph:       '◌',
    73	  color:       '#8888aa',
    74	  glow:        '#6666aa',
    75	  scale:       0.45,
    76	  material:    'iron',
    77	  rarity:      'uncommon',           // 'common' | 'uncommon' | 'rare' | 'epic' | 'legendary'
    78	  bonuses:     { maxHp: 5, defense: 1 },
    79	  weight:      0.06,
    80	  description: 'A plain iron band that makes you feel more resilient.',
    81	});
    82	```
    83	
    84	`type` drives everything:
    85	
    86	| type | catalogKind | slot |
    87	|---|---|---|
    88	| `weapon` | equipment | weapon |
    89	| `armor` | equipment | armor |
    90	| `helm` / `head` | equipment | head |
    91	| `belt` | equipment | belt |
    92	| `gloves` | equipment | gloves |
    93	| `legs` | equipment | legs |
    94	| `boots` | equipment | feet |
    95	| `ring` | equipment | ring |
    96	| `neck` / `amulet` | equipment | neck |
    97	| `offhand` / `shield` | equipment | offhand |
    98	| `ranged` | equipment | ranged |
    99	| `potion` | magic | — |
   100	| `scroll` | magic | — |
   101	| `wand` | magic | — |
   102	| `spellbook` | magic | — |
   103	| `food` | food | — |
   104	| `ingredient` | magic | — |
   105	| `material` | magic | — |
   106	| `tool` | magic | — |
   107	| `fuel` | magic | — |
   108	| `junk` | magic | — |
   109	
   110	---
   111	
   112	## Weapons
   113	
   114	```js

    49	/**

   104	export function defineItem(id, def) {

     2	// Central content registry. All defineItem() / defineMonster() calls
     3	// register their compiled outputs here. installContent() reads from here

     6	/** @type {Map<string, object>} id → compiled catalog-compatible item def */
     7	const _items = new Map();

    15	// ── Items ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    16	
    17	export function registerItem(id, compiledDef) {
    18	  if (_items.has(id)) {

    23

    29	export function registerMonster(id, compiledDef) {
    30	  if (_monsters.has(id)) {

    32	  }
    33	  _monsters.set(id, compiledDef);

    35

    40

```

> AGENT

The content layer is a strong example of scale management without static types: it collapses catalog, palette, and hook registration into validated DSL calls, and then tests the compiler behavior directly. That gives agents one obvious place to add content and immediate failure modes when required fields drift.

   348	 * @param {number} [def.defense=0]

   352	 * // Physics

   358	 * @param {number} [def.visionRange]
   359	 * @param {string} [def.aggro] - "passive" | default
   360	 * @param {boolean} [def.packSense]

   363	 * @param {boolean} [def.ambush]

   365	 * @param {number} [def.polymorphResistance] - 0..1 chance to reject a polymorph attempt before transformation.
   366	 * @param {string|number} [def.polymorphStability] - serialized body coherence enum: "unstable"|"ordinary"|"anchored"|"fixed". Legacy numbers normalize to that enum.
   367	 *
   368	 * // Resistances
   369	 * @param {object} [def.resistances]
   370	 * @param {string[]} [def.immune] - shorthand: auto-sets resistance mult to 0
   371	 * @param {string[]} [def.vulnerable] - shorthand: auto-sets resistance mult to 2
   372	 *
   373	 * // Equipment
   374	 * @param {Array} [def.wielding]
   375	 * @param {Array} [def.equipped]
   376	 * @param {Array} [def.inventory]
   377	 *
   378	 * // Spells

   382	 *

   384	 * @param {string} [def.lootTable]
   385	 * @param {number} [def.corpseDropChance]
   386	 * @param {string} [def.goreType]
   387	 *
   388	 * // Scripted hooks (raw callback arrays, compatible with existing system)
   389	 * @param {object} [def.hooks] - { onHit: [...], onDamaged: [...], onDeath: [...], whileLOS: [...] }
   390	 *
   391	 * // Metadata
   392	 * @param {object} [def.meta]
   393	 *
   394	 * @returns {string} The registered monster id
   395	 */
   396	export function defineMonster(id, def) {
   397	  if (!id || typeof id !== 'string') throw new Error('[defineMonster] id is required');
   398	  if (!def || typeof def !== 'object') throw new Error('[defineMonster] def is required');
   399	  if (!def.name) throw new Error(`[defineMonster "${id}"] name is required`);
   400	
   401	  const resistances = _buildResistances(def);
   402	
   403	  const monsterDef = {
   404	    id,
   405	    name: def.name,
   406	    tags: def.tags || [],
   407	    tier: def.tier ?? 0,
   408	    intelligence: def.intelligence ?? 3,
   409	    baseHp: def.hp ?? 10,
   410	    hpPerLevel: def.hpPerLevel ?? 1,
   411	    attack: def.attack ?? 1,
   412	    defense: def.defense ?? 0,
   413	    damageDice: def.damageDice || '1d4',
   414	    sizeClass: def.sizeClass || 'M',
   415	    massKg: def.massKg ?? 60,
   416	    resistances,
   417	    speed: def.speed ?? 1,
   418	    description: def.description || def.name,
   419	  };
   420	
   421	  // Optional AI fields
   422	  if (def.visionRange != null) monsterDef.visionRange = def.visionRange;
   423	  if (def.aggro) monsterDef.aggro = def.aggro;
   424	  if (def.packSense != null) monsterDef.packSense = def.packSense;
   425	  if (def.packRadius != null) monsterDef.packRadius = def.packRadius;
   426	  if (def.retreatHpPct != null) monsterDef.retreatHpPct = def.retreatHpPct;
   427	  if (def.ambush != null) monsterDef.ambush = def.ambush;
   428	  if (def.polymorphResistance != null) monsterDef.polymorphResistance = clamp(Number(def.polymorphResistance || 0), 0, 1);
   429	  if (def.polymorphStability != null) monsterDef.polymorphStability = normalizePolymorphStability(def.polymorphStability);
   430	  if (typeof def.sleep === 'string') {
   431	    monsterDef.sleep = def.sleep;
   432	  } else if (def.sleep && typeof def.sleep === 'object') {
   433	    monsterDef.sleep = { ...def.sleep };
   434	  }
   435	
   436	  // Equipment
   437	  if (def.wielding) monsterDef.wielding = def.wielding;
   438	  if (def.equipped) monsterDef.equipped = def.equipped;
   439	  if (def.inventory) monsterDef.inventory = def.inventory;
   440	
   441	  // Spells
   442	  if (def.learnedSpellIds) monsterDef.learnedSpellIds = def.learnedSpellIds;
   443	  if (def.maxMana != null) monsterDef.maxMana = def.maxMana;
   444	  if (def.manaRegen != null) monsterDef.manaRegen = def.manaRegen;
   445	
   446	  // Loot
   447	  if (def.lootTable) monsterDef.lootTable = def.lootTable;
   448	  if (def.corpseDropChance != null) monsterDef.corpseDropChance = def.corpseDropChance;
   449	  if (def.goreType) monsterDef.goreType = def.goreType;
   450	
   451	  // ── Hooks ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   452	  // Merge raw callback arrays (existing pattern) with compiled DSL hooks.
   453	  const hooks = def.hooks ? { ...def.hooks } : {};
   454	  _compileMonsterDslHooks(id, def, hooks);

     1	---
     2	name: using-entire
     3	description: >
     4	  Use for any codebase exploration or understanding task — reads historical
     5	  intent from Entire checkpoints instead of guessing. Orchestrates other Entire
     6	  skills to give the agent provenance-backed answers about code.
     7	---
     8	
     9	# Using Entire
    10	
    11	Your default when exploring or understanding code should be: **read the
    12	recorded intent, don't guess.**
    13	
    14	Entire checkpoints capture the prompts, transcripts, and decisions behind every
    15	agent-driven change. When you need to understand why code exists or how a module
    16	evolved, look up the checkpoint history first. Only fall back to inference from
    17	code structure when no history is available — and label that explicitly.
    18	
    19	## When This Skill Activates
    20	
    21	Use this skill whenever the task involves:
    22	
    23	- Exploring or understanding an unfamiliar module or file
    24	- Preparing to refactor, extend, or debug code you didn't write
    25	- Answering "why is this like this?" or "what was the intent?"
    26	- Doing pre-work research before making changes
    27	- Any codebase exploration where historical context would help
    28	
    29	Do **not** use this skill for simple, well-understood edits where you already
    30	have full context (e.g. "add a comment to line 5").
    31	
    32	## First Step: Check Repository Status
    33	
    34	Before diving into specific lookups, check whether Entire is available and
    35	the repository has checkpoint history:
    36	
    37	```bash
    38	entire status
    39	```
    40	
    41	This tells you whether Entire is enabled, which git hooks are installed, whether
    42	the metadata branch is initialized, and any active sessions on the current
    43	branch.
    44	
    45	## Scenario Routing
    46	
    47	When this skill activates, determine which sub-skill best fits the user's need:
    48	
    49	| Scenario | Typical user expressions | Delegate to |
    50	|----------|------------------------|-------------|
    51	| Code block provenance | "why is this like this" / "wtf is going on" / "what happened at src/auth.ts:42" / "tell me why this changed" | `what-happened` |
    52	| Find prior work | "has anyone done X before" / "search past work for rate limiting" / "find the previous implementation" | `search` |
    53	| Understand original intent | "explain this function" / "explain parseConfig" / "what was the intent behind src/auth.ts" | `explain` |
    54	| Generate a dispatch / summary | "summarize recent work" / "generate a dispatch" / "what was accomplished this week" | run `entire dispatch` directly |
    55	| Code review with intent context | "review these changes" / "review this branch" / "audit before merging" | `review` |
    56	| Hand off to another agent | "hand off this session" / "continue in another agent" / "pick up the codex session" | `session-handoff` |
    57	| Convert session to skill | "turn this into a skill" / "make a skill from this session" | `session-to-skill` |
    58	| Link session to other repos | "link session to other repos" / "attach session to the foo repo" | `session-crosslink` |
    59	| General exploration | "understand this module" / "explore how auth works" / "help me understand the codebase" | _(self — see below)_ |
    60	
    61	If the scenario clearly maps to a sub-skill, delegate entirely to that skill's
    62	workflow. Do not duplicate their steps here.
    63	
    64	## General Exploration Flow
    65	
    66	When no specific sub-skill fits — e.g. the user asks to "understand this
    67	module" or "explore how auth works" — use this flow:
    68	
    69	1. **Identify key files** in the target area (entry points, main types, core
    70	   functions).
    71	
    72	2. **Check for checkpoint coverage** on each key file:
    73	
    74	```bash
    75	git log --format='%H %s' -5 -- <file>
    76	```
    77	
    78	Look for `Entire-Checkpoint:` trailers in the commit bodies:
    79	
    80	```bash
    81	git log --format='%H %b' -5 -- <file> | grep -B1 'Entire-Checkpoint:'
    82	```
    83	
    84	3. **Read intent for covered commits** — extract the checkpoint ID from the
    85	   trailer, then use JSON output for non-interactive consumption:
    86	
    87	```bash
    88	entire explain --checkpoint <checkpoint-id> --json --no-pager
    89	```
    90	
    91	If you only have a commit hash (not a checkpoint ID), use:
    92	
    93	```bash
    94	entire explain --commit <sha> --no-pager
    95	```
    96	
    97	4. **Synthesize**: combine the recorded intent (from checkpoints) with the
    98	   current code structure to build a comprehensive understanding.
    99	
   100	5. **Report** your findings, clearly distinguishing:
   101	   - Facts backed by checkpoint transcripts (label as "recorded intent")
   102	   - Observations inferred from code alone (label as "inferred from code")
   103	
   104	## AI Agent Usage Notes
   105	
   106	When running Entire commands from an agent subprocess:
   107	
   108	- **Always pass `--json`** for commands that support it and might prompt
   109	  interactively. `--json` implies non-interactive output.
   110	- **Always pass `--no-pager`** to prevent pager activation in subprocess
   111	  contexts.
   112	- **Always specify `--checkpoint <id>` or `--commit <sha>`** for
   113	  `entire explain` — without a locator, the command falls back to an
   114	  interactive picker.
   115	
   116	## When No Checkpoints Exist
   117	
   118	If the target code predates Entire or has no checkpoint coverage:
   119	
   120	1. Say explicitly: "No Entire checkpoint history is available for this code."
   121	2. Proceed with standard code understanding (read source, check git log, trace
   122	   call sites).
   123	3. Label all explanations as "inferred from code — not backed by recorded
   124	   intent."
   125	4. If the user wants deeper history, suggest `search` with related keywords —
   126	   there may be checkpoints on related work even if the specific file isn't
   127	   directly covered.
   128	
   129	## Transparency Principle
   130	
   131	Always make it clear whether your answer comes from:
   132	
   133	- **Checkpoint-backed history** — you read the actual session transcript and
   134	  know the developer's stated intent
   135	- **Code inference** — you analyzed the code structure and are reasoning about
   136	  probable intent
   137	
   138	Never present inferred intent as if it were recorded fact. The core value of
   139	this workflow is that distinction.
   140	
   141	## References
   142	
   143	For a deeper understanding of Entire's capabilities beyond what the sub-skills
   144	cover, consult these resources:
   145	
   146	- **CLI help**: run `entire help` to see all available commands and flags
   147	- **Documentation**: https://docs.entire.io/llms.txt — AI-friendly
   148	  documentation index following the [llms.txt standard](https://llmstxt.org),
   149	  designed to be consumed directly by LLMs and coding agents

     2	name: search
     3	description: Use when the user wants to find prior work, checkpoints, or agent conversations by topic, repo, branch, author, or recent time window
     4	---
     5	
     6	# Search Checkpoints
     7	
     8	Use `entire search` to find relevant checkpoints before guessing from memory.
     9	
    10	## Response Format
    11	
    12	Begin the first response to this skill invocation with the line:
    13	
    14	`Entire Search:`
    15	
    16	followed by a blank line, then the content.
    17	
    18	- Apply the header to the **first response of the invocation only.** Do not re-print it on follow-up turns within the same invocation (e.g. after the user answers a clarifying question).
    19	- Do **not** include the header on error or early-exit responses (e.g. "Entire CLI not installed", "authentication required", "no matches"). The header's presence should signal that the skill ran and produced real output.
    20	
    21	## When to Use
    22	
    23	- The user asks things like "have we done this before?", "search past work", "find the previous implementation", or "look for checkpoints about X"
    24	- You need prior context from another branch, repo, author, or recent time period
    25	- You want likely matches first, then a deeper transcript read only for the best hit
    26	
    27	Do not use this for the current active session. Use `session-handoff` for that.
    28	
    29	## Process
    30	
    31	1. Run a focused search with JSON output so results are easy to inspect:
    32	
    33	```bash
    34	entire search "<query>" --json
    35	```
    36	
    37	Add filters when the user already gave them or when the first search is too broad:
    38	
    39	```bash
    40	entire search "<query>" --json --repo owner/name --branch branch-name --author "Name" --date week
    41	```
    42	
    43	Inline filters are also supported in the query: `author:<name>`, `date:<week|month>`, `branch:<name>`, `repo:<owner/name>`, `repo:*`.
    44	
    45	2. Review the top matches and summarize the likely candidates for the user. Do not dump raw JSON unless they ask for it.
    46	
    47	3. If the user wants details on a specific result, open the checkpoint with:
    48	
    49	```bash
    50	entire explain --checkpoint <checkpoint-id> --full --no-pager
    51	```
    52	
    53	If `--full` fails, fall back to:
    54	
    55	```bash
    56	entire explain --checkpoint <checkpoint-id> --raw-transcript --no-pager
    57	```
    58	
    59	## Search Heuristics
    60	
    61	- Start with the user's domain terms, feature name, error text, file name, or ticket ID
    62	- Prefer narrower searches before increasing `--limit`
    63	- Add `--repo` or `repo:*` explicitly when repository scope matters
    64	- If there are no useful hits, broaden in this order: remove branch filter, widen date, simplify query terms
    65	
    66	## Failure Modes
    67	
    68	- If search says authentication is required, tell the user to run `entire login`
    69	- If there are no matches, say that clearly and mention the filters or query terms you tried
    70	- If the user really wants the current session, switch to `session-handoff` instead of searching checkpoints

     1	---
     2	name: add-monster
     3	description: Add a new monster to the roguelike dungeon crawler
     4	argument-hint: <monster_id>
     5	disable-model-invocation: true
     6	---
     7	
     8	Add a new monster to `src/rules/data/monsters.js` with id `$ARGUMENTS`.
     9	
    10	## Steps
    11	
    12	1. **Ask the user** for the monster's concept if not obvious from the name — theme, tier, special abilities, flavor.
    13	2. **Read `src/rules/data/monsters.js`** to see current monsters and available callback imports.
    14	3. **Design the monster** following the schema and balance guidelines below.
    15	4. **Add the entry** to the `MONSTERS` array, placed within the correct tier comment block (Tier 0 / 1 / 2 / 3 / 99).
    16	5. **Add any new callback imports** at the top of the file if needed.
    17	6. **Add a palette entry** in `src/display/palette/base.js` (see Palette section below).
    18	7. **Run tests**: `deno test --allow-read` to verify nothing broke.
    19	
    20	## Required Fields (MonsterDef)
    21	
    22	Every monster object MUST have all of these fields:
    23	
    24	```
    25	id            string     — snake_case unique identifier
    26	name          string     — display name
    27	tags          string[]   — e.g. ['undead','skeletal'], ['beast','venomous'], ['humanoid']
    28	tier          number     — 0 (floors 1-5), 1 (floors 6-10), 2 (floors 11-15), 3 (floors 16+), 99 (special/untiered)
    29	intelligence  number     — 1-10 (drives AI behavior, see tiers below)
    30	baseHp        number     — starting HP
    31	hpPerLevel    number     — HP gained per dungeon depth
    32	attack        number     — attack bonus
    33	defense       number     — defense bonus
    34	damageDice    string     — e.g. '1d4', '2d6', '3d8'
    35	sizeClass     string     — 'XS', 'S', 'M', 'L', 'XL'
    36	massKg        number     — weight in kilograms
    37	resistances   object     — at minimum { kinetic: { DR: N } }; can add bluntMult, pierceMult, slashMult, chemical.toxMult, thermal.burnMult, electric.ohms
    38	speed         number     — 1 (slow) to 3 (fast); actEvery = max(1, 4-speed)
    39	hooks         object|null — combat/AI callback hooks, or null if none
    40	specials      string[]   — human-readable list of special abilities (shown in UI)
    41	description   string     — flavor text
    42	```
    43	
    44	## Optional Fields
    45	
    46	```
    47	aggro           'passive'   — won't attack on sight (only when struck)
    48	packSense       true        — alerts nearby same-species on sighting
    49	packRadius      number      — radius for pack alerting
    50	retreatHpPct    number      — flee threshold (0.0-1.0), e.g. 0.25 = flee at 25% HP
    51	ambush          true        — holds position until player within Chebyshev dist 1
    52	canFly          true        — can fly over terrain
    53	rare            true        — excluded from normal tier spawn pool
    54	visionRange     number      — override default vision (used by casters/ranged)
    55	lootTable       string      — e.g. 'drop:goblin', 'drop:tier2'; defaults to 'drop:tier{tier}'
    56	equipment       object      — { ranged: 'bow_short', ammo: 'arrows' } for ranged mobs
    57	learnedSpellIds string[]    — spell IDs the monster can cast
    58	maxMana         number      — mana pool (required if learnedSpellIds set)
    59	manaRegen       number      — mana regen per turn (required if learnedSpellIds set)
    60	```
    61	
    62	## Intelligence Tiers (AI behavior)
    63	
    64	- **1-3 (dumb)**: scurry system — wanders randomly when unaware (50% rest chance)
    65	- **4-5 (dim)**: basic chase, pack behavior
    66	- **6-7 (cunning)**: tactical — retreat, ambush capable
    67	- **8-9 (smart)**: predator — retreat + advanced positioning
    68	- **10 (sapient)**: picks up floor weapons if humanoid tag + unarmed
    69	
    70	## Available Callback Imports
    71	
    72	From `./callbacks/combat.js`:
    73	- `statusEffectOnHit(chance%, salt, { key, turnsLeft, potency }, procEvent)` — apply status on hit
    74	- `selfBuffOnHit({ key, turnsLeft, potency })` — self-buff when hitting
    75	- `drainOnHit(chance%, salt, amount)` — drain HP on hit
    76	- `bonusDamageOnBeforeHit(chance%, salt, amount, procEvent)` — bonus damage before hit
    77	- `bonusDamageIfTargetAfflicted(amount, statusKeys[], procEvent)` — bonus vs afflicted
    78	- `healOnDamaged(chance%, salt, amount, procEvent)` — self-heal when taking damage
    79	- `retaliateOnDamaged(amount, procEvent)` — reflect damage
    80	- `statusEffectOnDamaged(chance%, salt, { key, turnsLeft, potency }, procEvent, isSelf)` — status on taking damage
    81	- `phaseOutOnDamaged(chance%, salt)` — dodge/phase out when hit
    82	- `mindflayerBlastOnHit(chance%, salt)` — psychic blast on hit
    83	
    84	From `./callbacks/ai.js`:
    85	- `selfThrowNearTargetOnSeen({ searchRadius, fallbackSearchRadius, cooldownTurns, chance })` — web throw on sight
    86	- `gazeOnLOS(minRange, maxRange, actEvery)` — gaze stun mechanic
    87	- `fireBreathLineOnLOS({ minRange, maxRange, cooldownTurns, damage, hazardDamage, hazardTurns, burnTurns, burnPotency })` — fire breath
    88	- `castSpellOnLOS({ spellId, minRange?, maxRange?, cooldownTurns, chance, targeting?, maxAlliesInRadius?, allyRadius? })` — cast spell in LOS
    89	
    90	From `./callbacks/death.js`:
    91	- `spawnPlasmaCloudOnDeath({ turnsLeft, radius, damage })` — AoE on death
    92	
    93	## Hook Slots
    94	
    95	```
    96	onHit       — fires when this monster hits a target
    97	onBeforeHit — fires before damage is applied
    98	onDamaged   — fires when this monster takes damage
    99	onSeen      — fires when player first enters LOS
   100	whileLOS    — fires every turn while player is in LOS (for gaze, breath, spells)
   101	onDeath     — fires when this monster dies
   102	```
   103	
   104	## Salt Convention
   105	
   106	Each `statusEffectOnHit` / similar callback needs a unique hex salt for deterministic RNG. Use the pattern `0xdead####` where `####` is an unused hex value. Check existing monsters for used salts and pick a new one.
   107	
   108	## Balance Guidelines by Tier
   109	
   110	| Tier | baseHp | attack | defense | DR  | damageDice |
   111	|------|--------|--------|---------|-----|------------|
   112	| 0    | 3-28   | 0-4    | 0-3     | 0-10| 1d2-2d6    |
   113	| 1    | 8-15   | 1-3    | 0-2     | 2-6 | 1d4-1d8    |
   114	| 2    | 13-30  | 3-5    | 1-4     | 4-14| 1d6-2d8    |
   115	| 3    | 28-50  | 4-6    | 4-6     | 6-18| 2d6-3d8    |
   116	
   117	## Palette Entry (`src/display/palette/base.js`)
   118	
   119	Every monster needs a palette entry so it renders on screen. Add to the correct tier section.
   120	
   121	```js
   122	my_monster: { glyph: "M", fg: "#ff4444", glow: "#cc0000" },
   123	```
   124	
   125	### Fields
   126	- `glyph` — single ASCII character (or emoji for special cases)
   127	- `fg` — foreground hex color (what the player sees)
   128	- `glow` — glow/shadow hex color (slightly darker/muted version of fg)
   129	
   130	### Glyph Conventions
   131	| Type          | Glyph | Examples                               |
   132	|---------------|-------|----------------------------------------|
   133	| Humanoid S    | lowercase | g=goblin, k=kobold, o=orc          |
   134	| Humanoid L    | UPPERCASE | O=ogre, T=troll, L=lich            |
   135	| Beast small   | lowercase | r=rat, b=bat, x=spider/bug         |
   136	| Beast large   | UPPERCASE | D=dragon, S=snake                  |
   137	| Skeletal      | s     | skeleton, skeleton_archer, bone_bowman |
   138	| Spectral      | W     | wraith                                 |
   139	| Demon         | &     | demon                                  |
   140	| Aberration    | e     | floating_eye                           |
   141	| Mimic         | M     | mimic                                  |
   142	| Construct     | #     | stone_taunter                          |
   143	
   144	### Color Families
   145	| Theme     | fg example  | glow example |
   146	|-----------|-------------|--------------|
   147	| Bone/undead | #ddd8c8   | #aaa590      |
   148	| Green/orc | #cc6644     | #993320      |
   149	| Purple/magic | #cc88ff  | #9955cc      |
   150	| Fire/red  | #ff4444     | #cc0000      |
   151	| Ice/blue  | #aabbff     | #7799dd      |
   152	| Electric/yellow | #ffff66 | #cccc33    |
   153	| Poison/green | #55bb55  | #338833      |
   154	
   155	Corpses are auto-generated by `buildPalette()` — all monsters inherit their fg/glow with a `%` glyph.
   156	
   157	## Rules
   158	
   159	- JavaScript only — no TypeScript.
   160	- Use `world.rand()` for RNG, never `Math.random()`.
   161	- Place the entry in the correct tier section of the MONSTERS array.
   162	- If adding a new callback type, import it at the top of the file.
   163	- Always add a matching palette entry in `src/display/palette/base.js`.
   164	- Run `deno test --allow-read` after adding the monster.

```

> AGENT

**Identified Techniques**

1. **Agent field manual at repo root**
The provided `AGENTS.md` is not generic. It gives agents hard laws, command recipes, architecture maps, target lookup commands, event-bus commands, and merge checklist items. That reduces exploration cost and prevents agents from inventing local doctrine.

2. **Named guardrail tasks**
[Deno tasks](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/deno.json:8) package architecture, event, death-pipeline, tooling, health, and ratchet checks into stable commands. The important bit is that agents do not need to reconstruct “what should I run?” from scratch: `guard:architecture`, `guard:events`, `guard:tools`, `ratchet:world-state`, etc. are explicit.

3. **Executable architecture health report**
[tools/agent-health.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tools/agent-health.mjs:123) scans for boundary violations, `emitSafe`, nondeterminism hazards, system-to-system calls, and system counts, then formats an “agent health” summary. This is custom static analysis aimed exactly at agent failure modes.

4. **Agent tools are tested**
[tests/agentTools.test.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tests/agentTools.test.mjs:27) tests the event-bus explorer, system-map parser, import-boundary classifier, target search ranking, content-id classifier, and health report formatting. The project does not just ship helper scripts; it protects their behavior.

5. **Event-bus explorer**
[tools/event-bus-explorer.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tools/event-bus-explorer.mjs:1) extracts literal/dynamic `world.emit`, `world.on`, `ctx.io.emit`, producer/consumer counts, payload keys, orphan producers, and orphan consumers. For a JS ECS project without types, this gives agents a queryable behavioral graph.

6. **System map**
[tools/system-map.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tools/system-map.mjs:1) parses scheduler imports and `registerSystem(...)` calls, reports phase placement, unregistered system files, and missing obvious tests. That makes scheduler-scale complexity inspectable.

7. **Targeted search tools**
[tools/agent-target.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tools/agent-target.mjs:1) ranks matches by event, symbol, file, import, and text. [tools/content-id-audit.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tools/content-id-audit.mjs:1) follows item/monster/spell/material IDs through definitions, visuals, spawn/loot, events, references, and tests. These are agent-specific navigation aids.

8. **Hard layer separation, documented and tested**
[docs/architecture/SEPARATION_MANIFEST.md](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/SEPARATION_MANIFEST.md:17) defines allowed imports between `rules`, `bridge`, `display`, `shared`, and `app`. [tests/separationBoundaries.test.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tests/separationBoundaries.test.mjs:1) enforces key import boundaries.

9. **Determinism as an architectural primitive**
The separation doc says the same seed and intent sequence should produce identical state/events [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/SEPARATION_MANIFEST.md:32). [tests/determinismArchitectureGuards.test.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tests/determinismArchitectureGuards.test.mjs:1) bans time/random APIs in deterministic layers. This is crucial for agent debugging because behavior can be replayed.

10. **Central scheduler with explicit phases**
[src/main/scheduler.js](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/main/scheduler.js:126) wires all systems in one composition root, with phases like `ai`, `intents`, `effects`, `scripts`, and `cleanup`; it finally composes them at [line 320](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/main/scheduler.js:320). Agents can reason about order without scanning every system for implicit calls.

11. **Order constraints are local and visible**
Scheduler registration uses `before`/`after` constraints, for example movement before ranged actions and casting [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/main/scheduler.js:234). This replaces hidden inter-system coupling with inspectable scheduling.

12. **Data-driven content DSL**
[docs/CONTENT-AUTHORING.md](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/CONTENT-AUTHORING.md:21) says one `defineItem` call validates inputs, infers catalog kind, compiles hooks, and registers palette atomically. [src/content/define.js](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:104) implements this for items; [line 396](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:396) implements monsters.

13. **Import-time validation instead of static types**
`defineItem` throws for missing `id`, `def`, `name`, or `type` [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:104). Tests assert those failures [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/tests/contentDsl.test.mjs:262). That partially substitutes for TypeScript by making bad content fail early.

14. **One content definition registers multiple projections**
`defineItem` registers the catalog entry [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:276), palette [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:279), presentations [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:290), and abilities [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/define.js:297). This reduces ID drift across systems.

15. **Central duplicate-detecting content registry**
[src/content/registry.js](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/registry.js:17) rejects duplicate item IDs, and [line 29](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/content/registry.js:29) rejects duplicate monster IDs. That protects agents from silently shadowing content.

16. **Concrete doctrine for avoiding hidden runtime state**
[docs/architecture/RUNTIME_TOPOLOGY_DOCTRINE.md](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/RUNTIME_TOPOLOGY_DOCTRINE.md:15) says runtime things with identity/lifecycle/debug relevance should be entities, not arrays. It also names the compatibility/migration approach [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/RUNTIME_TOPOLOGY_DOCTRINE.md:146).

17. **Ratchets for known debt**
[ratchets/worldStateAttachmentRatchet.test.mjs](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/ratchets/worldStateAttachmentRatchet.test.mjs:23) carries an allowance list for existing `world[...]` state, then fails if counts grow [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/ratchets/worldStateAttachmentRatchet.test.mjs:89). This lets agents improve incrementally without reopening old debt.

18. **Negative-history docs**
[docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md:3) records past derailments, then converts them into hard rules: tests before systems [line 36](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md:36), one new system per session [line 48](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md:48), data over hard-coded systems [line 66](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md:66), no second scheduler [line 83](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/docs/architecture/TEN_COMMANDMENTS.md:83). This is unusually agent-useful because it captures traps, not just ideals.

19. **Vendored ECS is itself agent-oriented**
[src/lib/ecs-js/AGENTS.md](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/lib/ecs-js/AGENTS.md:7) explicitly explains why the ECS works for agents: deterministic core, caller-driven ticks, pure logic, composable helpers. It gives a step-and-explain workflow [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/src/lib/ecs-js/AGENTS.md:15).

20. **Entire provenance**
The repo has `.agents/skills/using-entire`, which tells agents to read recorded intent instead of guessing [here](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/.agents/skills/using-entire/SKILL.md:11). Git commits also carry `Entire-Checkpoint:` trailers; the last three commits I inspected all had them. This makes agent work historically searchable and explainable instead of ephemeral.

21. **Task-specific agent skills**
The repo has local skills like [add-monster](/home/pjensen/Repos/JSHack/.agents/skills/add-monster/SKILL.md:10), which gives a procedural playbook, required fields, balance tables, callback imports, salt conventions, palette rules, and test commands. That converts repeated single-author knowledge into reusable agent procedure.

The broad answer: agentic development is possible here because the project replaces missing static types and human-only memory with executable guardrails, deterministic replay, query tools, DSL validation, explicit architecture doctrine, debt ratchets, and recorded development provenance.
